Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the Universal Improvement Generator Database database, public edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records, each with its description, math proof, science proof, history, construction workflow, generator, AI, demo, and sources.

Search the records with the box below — it searches titles, descriptions, and subjects across all 24 records.

1. Descriptions

Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Proposals

JAH-UIG-000001 · Improvement Proposals

Description: This record specifies Improvement Proposals as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Evidence: documented — Records the full official specification for Improvement Proposals as part of the 100 God-Level resources catalog, with attribution kept straight: the catalog is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so.

Universal Improvement Generator: Controlled Trials

JAH-UIG-000004 · Controlled Trials

Description: This record specifies Controlled Trials as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is risks. 2. Math Proof

Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Proposals

JAH-UIG-000001 · Improvement Proposals

3. Science Proof

Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Proposals

JAH-UIG-000001 · Improvement Proposals

Science proof: The science behind this record is established practice, not a new claim. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of proposals, trials, kept changes, and rollbacks. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the record is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

4. History

Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Proposals

JAH-UIG-000001 · Improvement Proposals

History: The catalog arrived as pasted text on 2026-11-17, titled 100 God-Level / Boundless Civilization Resources for the JAH Network Master AI. The text reads as another AI's words, like the Foundation, College, PhD, NASA, DARPA, and Future catalogs before it; its source is NOT confirmed, and it is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The cross-check of all 100 items against the live manifest found this item, number 96, to be a genuine gap: no live or staged database covered it, so it was picked for Wave 1 as one of only eight genuine BUILD items, with no padding. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Dataset: 100 God-Level / Boundless Civilization Resources catalog, item #96, Universal Improvement Generator; cross-check BACKLOG.md, Wave 1 pick; sources retrieved 2026-11-17.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this record. First, this is a specification, not a deployed system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this specification has been built or tested at scale. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Proposals

JAH-UIG-000001 · Improvement Proposals

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Improvement Proposals inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Improvement Proposals as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Improvement Proposals that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Improvement Proposals inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Universal Improvement Generator: Baseline Measurement

JAH-UIG-000002 · Baseline Measurement

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Baseline Measurement inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Baseline Measurement as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Comparison Against Baseline that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Comparison Against Baseline inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Universal Improvement Generator: Rollback Plans

JAH-UIG-000006 · Rollback Plans

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Rollback Plans inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Rollback Plans as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Universal Improvement Generator: Cost-Benefit Review

JAH-UIG-000010 · Cost-Benefit Review

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Cost-Benefit Review inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Cost-Benefit Review as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for The Improvement Backlog that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach The Improvement Backlog inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Universal Improvement Generator: Root-Cause Analysis

JAH-UIG-000014 · Root-Cause Analysis

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Root-Cause Analysis inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Root-Cause Analysis as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Universal Improvement Generator: Knowledge Capture

JAH-UIG-000018 · Knowledge Capture

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Knowledge Capture inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Knowledge Capture as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Knowledge Capture that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Knowledge Capture inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Maturity

JAH-UIG-000022 · Improvement Maturity

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Improvement Maturity inside the Universal Improvement Generator. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Method: This record specifies Improvement Maturity as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. Scope: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Improvement Maturity that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Improvement Maturity inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

6. Generator

Each record carries the same boundless generator: it makes full records on demand; generated content is labeled simulated for weeding and double-checked.

7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Improvement Librarian. It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Improvement Librarian).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss Universal Improvement Generator Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.

8. Demo

Every record in this database carries a working demo program and a boundless generator. The demo for this website is demo.js — run it with node demo.js. It searches the edition's real 24 records and runs a deterministic generator demo (seed 7) over them. The run output is saved in demo-test.txt and shown below, exactly as executed — this demo actually ran.

DATABASE: universal-improvement-generator
EDITION: public
RECORDS: 24
SEARCH "universal": 24 hit(s)
  - JAH-UIG-000001 :: Universal Improvement Generator: Improvement Proposals
  - JAH-UIG-000002 :: Universal Improvement Generator: Baseline Measurement
